ParlaMint-SI_2012-12-05-SDZ6-Izredna-23.ana.u1	Dear colleagues and colleagues, distinguished ministers and ministers, welcome! We continue the 23rd extraordinary meeting of the National Assembly. The following Members and Members are unable to attend today's meeting: mag. Melita Župevc, Janez Vasle and Roberto Battelli. We're moving on to one. AND 2. On the day-to-day basis, this is at the joint introduction of the supplementary draft budget of the Republic of Slovenia for 2013 and the supplementary draft budget of the Republic of Slovenia for 2014. The Government submitted the supplementary draft budgets for consideration to the National Assembly. To further explain both proposals, I give the floor to the representative of the Government, Minister Janez Šuštersič.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-12-05-SDZ6-Izredna-23.ana.u2	Dear Members and Members, President and colleagues and colleague from the Government! Allow me to briefly present the updated draft budget in the introduction. This, however, is not very different from what we have proposed as the basic draft budgets for the next two years, so my initial presentation will not be significantly different, except in the sense that some of the things that were still potential when the draft budget was tabled, in particular the tax and pension reform changes, are now adopted and are no longer an assumption on which the budget is based, but are, in other words, a fact. The general logic of the draft budgets is to keep the commitment. We plan budgets by maintaining the commitment with them to bring the deficit in the coming year of the state budget below 3% of gross domestic product in order to achieve this objective. We have assessed that this objective remains important, despite the acute economic situation, in particular in order to demonstrate the credibility of the country outside and to avoid becoming involved in procedures where we would withdraw from these commitments. This objective is to be achieved through a combination of three measures. One is measures to reduce expenditure. Secondly, measures to increase the revenue of the budget and, most importantly, together then give us room for strong development incentives. Let me tell you something about this first. The budget proposal for next year foresees approximately 600 million more investment expenditure. To a large extent, these are investments that are linked or co-financed by European funds and programmes that can be co-financed by such funds. It is not just infrastructure, it is also environmental projects, education, social policy in one part or employment policy. In addition, there are investments financed directly from the budget. In particular, in the area of health, they will be more than this year, when we have largely stopped or slowed down these projects due to the adjustment of budget expenditure. It is also important that the draft budget takes into account the important tax incentives that have been adopted in recent months. It is the flat-rate taxation of small entrepreneurs, small businesses and people who carry out their own business. This is a reduction in the rate of corporate income tax. This year this rate is 18%, next year it will be 17%, in two years 16%. This is an unlimited investment relief and it is about increasing the facilitation of investment in R & D. Estimates of how much less revenue there will be in the budget are uncertain. One such working assessment, together, lowering rates and additional incentives for investment, speaks of about 200 million – this is reflected in the budget. What is important about this is that it is not 200 million that will blow up or not be in the budget, but it is 200 million that will remain in the economy, for those companies that invest in R & D, in investments, and who create profits, and therefore can enforce this relief or this reduced rate of profit tax. For those companies that are still living, not only living, but also expanding their investment activity, this money will remain and will be used there for economic development, not only for the investments of these companies, but also for the opening up of jobs that are always made possible by expanding economic activity. We also changed the income scale by moving the middle class, where the middle class limit begins, from 1.3 to 1.5 average wages. This is still low, but corresponds to at least approximately the average income of people with high education, so that a significant segment of the population is partially or to some extent taxly decommissioned and we give them additional incentives for activity. On the expense side. Yesterday's adopted pension reform, according to estimates made at the time of the drafting of the law, is expected to amount to approximately 150 million savings in the budget. We are counting on a reduction in the resources for the mass of wages and other labour costs, around 5%, slightly less, given what was adopted in this year's balance sheet. In addition, there will be a need to save what will be exceeded this year's balance because the measures were not as strong as we originally planned in the area of wage cuts this year in the public sector. This is a total of around 300 million financial impact. We reduce the consumption of municipalities by reducing the average and by reducing the specific transfer to the capital. In total, this is about 40 million positive effects on budget revenues or on lower expenditure. We are also continuing the overall cost-saving planning of the country's material costs and other current expenditure. There are also many measures on the revenue side that raise certain tax burdens. Their overall impact is around €300 million. Most of this, I think virtually everything, has already been adopted in the National Assembly. It is the introduction of a tax on financial services and the tightening of the parameters of the tax on the balance sheet sum of banks. This is about increasing certain environmental duties, including the burden of more powerful and more expensive motor vehicles, which is the form of a property tax, if we can call it that. We introduced a special CO2 tax in fuel. This is an increase in the level of concession paid from student work, which means 30 million additional assigned revenue for scholarships. This is a temporary introduction of a 50 % rate of income to increase the rate of tax paid on capital income from 20 % to 25 %. The same applies to new income from the lease. This is to tighten the anti-crisis real estate tax, where we lower the limit from 1 million to 500 thousand property values. This is a planned increase in excise duties announced by the introduction of sweet drinks taxation, replacing the withdrawal of the proposal to change tax rates to value added tax. It is also a matter of measures against the grey economy, where, in our opinion, the publication of taxpayers, which has already been legislated by amendments to the Tax Procedure Act, will play an important role. Let me get my numbers back. This is a combination of three types of measures – approximately 400 million savings in expenditure, about 300 million additional revenues with the measures I have counted. This, on the one hand, allows us to plan a deficit of 2.8% of gross domestic product while at the same time providing some 800 million incentives to the economy through investment projects, European co-financing and through the tax reliefs I have already mentioned. This is a budget that operates on all three sides, both on income and on expenditure, provides economic incentives and, in our view, it will not have a negative impact on economic growth, as we said this year when we adopt the balance that deficit reduction represents a short-term negative impact on economic growth. At the time, the assessment was about half a percentage point of gross domestic product growth. Finally, let me say something else about the obvious or the question whether such a budget is real or not. Let me say again about the starting point for this year. This year we will end up in the framework of the rebalancing proposed and in the framework of the deficit allowed by the Budget Implementation Act for this year, with the exception of those loans that have been made during the year for the recapitalisation of the New Ljubljana Bank, which will increase the deficit in the financing account. However, this deficit has already been covered by a reduction in the state's assets in the accounts, as we have explained at this recapitalisation of the New Ljubljana Bank of debt, which has already been made in previous years and has created reserves for this purpose. The pension reform, on which an important part of the measures on the expenditure side was based, was adopted yesterday in the National Assembly. According to the first statement, no referendum requirements are expected. Such impact assessments are always uncertain and, therefore, in the Law on the Implementation of the Budget, we have provided for a reserve that, should there be a difference this year between the mandatory expenditure of the pension fund laid down by law and the revenue from contributions and the planned transfer from the state budget, this difference could be covered by an additional transfer from the capital company. This is a security measure, it is not a pre-determined decision as to what this transfer will be, but it is a possibility that this difference, if it occurs, will be covered by this source. At the same time, it is important to foresee in the Slovenian State Holding Act, if not elsewhere, and also in the Budget Implementation Act that part of the purchases from state assets - in that law was foreseen 10% - will be transferred to the Capital Strengthening Fund, which means again that this long-term reserve of the pension fund is not reduced in this way, except perhaps in the short term or in a temporary way. As regards estimates that plans to reduce public sector wages are unrealistic and that we have not been coordinated sufficiently, I would like to say in particular the following. Through several rounds of coordination of the government's budget, the government's search for ways of saving, has come to a conclusion or calculation as to how much of the funds for wages and other labour costs should be reduced in order to reach the budget within the set frameworks. In doing so, we had more limitations, not only this foreseen framework of the 3% deficit, but also other constraints. One of the constraints that was very important was that we did not want to propose tightening the measures already taken in the Public Finance Balance Act, so we did not propose further wage cuts, nominal wage cuts in the public sector or measures in some of the other areas in which we have already intervened with the Public Finance Balance Act this year. We also did not, as I said in the introductory presentation, want to impose additional taxes on economies in order to maintain too much employment in the public sector, in our view. This has led us to this number, which is a target number included in the draft budgets for the next two years. This is a goal set out in clear financial constraints. In order to achieve this goal of reducing wages, various measures are available, not just job redundancies or job withdrawals. Different adjustments are also possible. Given that the situations in different parts of the public sector are different, the Government has taken this action by asking the heads of the public sector to work on scenarios, calculations, which measures are possible to reduce wages, wages. It's about organisational action. All would like forgiveness, except where justified for any other reason, to be a last resort. However, in order to achieve this, it is necessary to make programmes, plans, reorganisations, different systems of evaluating what kind of work, what kind of jobs. We would like to see a dialogue with the unions about this, but the unions have decided to take a different approach. They want to negotiate first about how much the pay will go down and only then how. Our view is that this process should be reversed. First of all, what are the options if we decide to make these adjustments and if we all realize that these adjustments are necessary, what measures are available, where are we going with them? Only then is it possible to negotiate a difference that may remain. In addition, let me say that this figure, that the amount of wage and other labour costs should be reduced by 5%, in view of the balance that has been adopted, should not surprise anyone. This year, when we proposed the 2012 budget rebalancing, we initially proposed a reduction, a nominal reduction in public sector wages by 15%, with the possibility of eliminating imbalances, which would effectively mean a 10% reduction in nominal wages if such a proposal were adopted. Then we should not have to propose any additional adjustments for next year in terms of wages and other labour costs. As you know, such a baseline proposal has not been adopted. In order to be able to take or defend other important measures, it was necessary to agree with the unions. It is very important that this agreement also contained the fact that nothing is done this year in the field of education standards, which are very important restrictions on flexibility in adapting labour costs in this part of the public sector. Because of the lack of or acceptance of what the Government has proposed – a 10% nominal reduction in wages – in this area, and in the end, we have achieved between 3% and 4% reduction in salaries this year, there was no other choice for next year than to propose an additional step of adjustment that is not as strong as those 10%, but is still strong. At the same time, we have opened the way for adaptation not only by reducing the actual wages that people receive but also by other organisational measures in the field of other labour costs and also by reducing employment. Certain rules will therefore have to be amended. Normatives in education, which have not been negotiated seriously, are certainly one of the important things that will need to be changed in order to achieve these objectives. As far as I am concerned about pension reform and the mass of wages, the third question raised with regard to the reality of the draft budget is the economic situation. As you know, we have bad economic forecasts for both this year and next year. We have come from the latest predictions of Umar, which were made in September and are still valid. Even with the latest publication of data for the third quarter on gross domestic product in Slovenia, the death assessment was that this is in line with their forecasts and that we are not expecting any serious changes in these forecasts, so at least at this point we can say that the budget is based on real macroeconomic foundations. One last thing. As you probably know, the European Commission's assessment of the deficit under the European methodology is slightly higher than the 3% we are talking about under the European account system in Slovenia. However, there are differences that are understandable. On the one hand, it is a security reserve if perhaps all the measures announced by the Government would not be implemented and, on the other hand, a slightly greater impact, a greater assessment of the negative impact of the economic situation on income. Nevertheless, yesterday we had another such conversation in Brussels, including with the European Commission and ECOFIN. Nevertheless, the European Commission considers that the adjustment to the measures taken this year in the field of public finances proposed for next year is such that they are in line with expectations. It is about adjusting the structural deficit by about a percentage and a half of gross domestic product this year and next year. This is what's most important, this structural adjustment. Despite several different assessments, yesterday the European Commission made it clear that there is no reason at this point for Slovenia to require any additional measures in the field of public finances. What we are doing is assessed as being in line with the country's commitments in the grave and leading in the public financial field in the right direction. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-12-05-SDZ6-Izredna-23.ana.u3	The updated draft budget for 2013 and the updated draft budget for 2014 was treated as a parent working body by the Committee on Finance and Monetary Policy. I give the floor to the chairman of the committee, mag. To present the report to Andre Shirtz.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-12-05-SDZ6-Izredna-23.ana.u4	Thank you very much for your word, Madam President. Dear Minister, distinguished ministers, colleagues and colleagues! At its 39th urgent meeting, the Committee on Finance and Monetary Policy, as a working parent, considered the updated draft budgets of the Republic of Slovenia for 2013 and 2014 submitted by the Government to the National Assembly for consideration. The Committee held a joint debate on the 2013 and 2014 budgets. He voted on the amendments for each act individually. For the 2013 budget, 11 amendments were forwarded to the Committee, which were submitted to the Social Democrats and the Positive Slovenia Group within the deadline for the supplementary draft budget. The Committee also received a written opinion from the Council of Justice on the 2013 and 2014 draft budgets. In a joint introductory presentation of the 2013 and 2014 supplementary budget proposals, the Minister of Finance presented new developments in the supplementary drafts of the budget for the next two years. He drew attention to an increase of EUR 3 million in revenue on the basis of changes in the additional income. On the expenditure side, they will also increase by €8 million, mainly because the funds are foreseen for the six-year extension of the AUKN. The Government has also provided an additional 5 million to operate the courts. The Government aims to maintain the deficit as planned, i.e. 2.8% of the gross social product in 2013 and 2.5% of the gross social product in 2014. On the Committee, the representative of Umarja said that they did not foresee any major deviations from the autumn forecasts, which meant the continuation of the poor economic situation and the recurrence of the gross social product by 1.4 % in 2013 and the modest growth of the gross social product in 2014. In its opening remarks, union representatives on the Committee expressed doubts that the 2013 and 2014 budgets could be implemented in the field of salaries and other public sector costs. They also pointed out that the Government had not negotiated on these union proposals. They also pointed out a significant reduction in resources for education, science and culture and highlighted the issue of pre-school education. They proposed to suspend the decision on the two supplementary draft budgets. Trade union representatives also pointed out that it was necessary to find those responsible for such a poor situation in the country, stressing the need to find those responsible for providing bad credits. The representative of the Council of Justice initially said that the judiciary was aware of the state's situation, but felt that the judiciary could not tolerate such drastic measures to reduce funds. They therefore independently proposed their part of the budget. She also presented efforts to rationalise the work of the courts. She said that, on the basis of the negotiations, there were up to €5 million in additional funding for the courts, which she welcomed. In the joint debate on the supplementary budget proposals for the two years, some discussers from opposition parliamentary groups felt that the complementary draft budgets were unrealistic and poorly prepared. They drew attention to the excessive reduction in resources for research and development, education, culture, active employment policy and the municipalities. They also considered that there was a lack of emphasis on the revenue side and a lack of incentives for large investments. They expressed doubts about the government's budget deficit forecast. They estimated that this would be larger, with an estimated value of about 5.5 % to 6 % of the gross social product for 2013. They also considered the debt assessment to be unrealistic and would reach up to around 70% of the gross social product over the years. At the end, they proposed postponement of the decision on the proposed budgets. Controversies from coalition parties, on the contrary, considered the draft budgets to be optimal and realistic and acceptable in terms of the situation.It is important that Slovenia does not wait for decisions, since this time is no longer. This is the first draft of the new government's budgets and it is important to promote economic growth and competitiveness. Members considered both draft budgets to be too restrictive. Following a final discussion on the 2013 State budget proposal, the Committee identified the amendments to the Parliamentary Groups of the Social Democrats and the Positive Slovenia tabled in accordance with Rule 160 of the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly. In the context of the definition of individual amendments, the Committee did not support any of the amendments tabled to the 2013 supplementary draft budget. The Committee also identified up to ten amendments to the parliamentary groups of Social Democrats and Positive Slovenia following the closure of the discussion on the 2014 supplementary draft budget of the Republic of Slovenia. These were also submitted in accordance with the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly. Within the framework of the definition of individual amendments, the Committee did not support any of the amendments tabled to the 2014 supplementary draft budget. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2012-12-05-SDZ6-Izredna-23.ana.u6	Thank you for your word, and I'd like to say a very warm hello to all of you. As every year, we are looking ahead to the budget, one of the most important and fundamental acts for the functioning of the country. This act usually causes much bad will and headaches, anger, excitement, for it tells us which area of our lives will be most affected. Hand to heart, the draft budget usually clearly indicates where it will be saved and cut. Even in the 2013 and 2014 supplementary drafts of the budget of the Republic of Slovenia, there is nothing different. We've been used to this since before. Thus, it is clear from both proposals that the tightening of the belt continues in all areas and that all of us, both the active population and pensioners, benefit from the savings. The pensioners are expected to feel this way by failing to reconcile their pensions at the level that has been the case in recent years or at all. There are also no investments envisaged, construction of new homes for old and other adaptations of infrastructure to the ageing Slovenian population. The active population, in particular public servants, will feel savings in terms of reducing the number of employees or lower wages, as the estimated wage for both years is about 10% lower compared to the realisation in 2012. We could still list the cases. The undeniable fact is that the 2013 and 2014 draft budgets of the Republic of Slovenia have been supplemented by significant cuts in all areas and allow for the adoption of different policy follow-up measures. The effects of these measures will be varied over the period when the budget itself will be deep in the implementation phase. The strategic objectives of working for Slovenia to continue to develop faster than the average of the European Union since 2013 in order to reduce the number of unemployed, to raise employment for the Lisbon objective, turn towards a negative direction, to increase unemployment. The economic and financial crisis has increased unemployment, household incomes have declined sharply, and the rate of poverty has also increased. The material condition of a large part of the population has deteriorated and more people are materially disadvantaged. The new legislation and austerity measures reduce social transfers, which is also reflected in both draft budgets. The fact is that the two supplementary draft budgets are based on certain legal bases that have not yet been or have not been fully harmonised and adopted, so there is a high likelihood in the near future that substantial additional revenue and expenditure adjustments, i.e. the rebalance. This is due to the effects that the proposed reforms will bring. The revenues in both proposals are too optimistic. In line with the agreement on the basic objective of ensuring a favourable environment for cultural growth and strengthening Slovenian and European identity, the lower rate of VAT for printing and the harmonisation of tax revenues were fortunately maintained. Fortunately, I have said that the country must act with discretion in the additional burden of the population in these times of crisis, not raise or introduce new taxes. In the DeSUS Group, we also assess that ambitious European revenue planning is not entirely realistic, since in the past the use of these funds in the Republic of Slovenia has not been successful enough. In doing so, I would like to draw attention to the failure of collection of taxes and contributions. There is something to talk about the expected tax revenues, and it is quite different to realize these revenues. To do this, you need to have an effective system, which we obviously do not have yet, so our share of the unpaid taxes is about 10% of the gross social product. For these purposes, Durs must be equipped with modern programming equipment for the ongoing collection and proper recording of citizens' obligations, thus affecting the real planning and implementation of the budget's expected revenues. In addition to the grey economy, Dursa’s great problem is also the recovery of the already imposed tax, and concrete and effective solutions should also be found in this area, which will result in no more taxpayers. This is what I think we have done in part by discussing lists that will, under certain conditions, show all debtors. That I will not only be negative, let me commend the fact that the 2013 budget for the Ministry of Health is increasing by 54.1 million euros, compared to this year. More resources than in 2012 are planned for secondary and tertiary protection and investment investments. There is also a higher budget for health care for unprotected and socially vulnerable people of approximately €2.6 million. Health policy follows the objective of regulating the country's healthcare system in the direction of balancing the social and economic development of the Republic of Slovenia. The activities are aimed at changing the healthcare system in a way that, despite widespread savings, retains a new level of health safety and ensures social justice, which we welcome in the Group of the Democratic Party of Pensioners of Slovenia. In 2013, activities will focus on increasing efficiency and effectiveness and improving the quality and safety of the entire healthcare system. Public health aims to prolong the health of the elderly and to reduce differences in geographical terms and in individual groups of citizens. At the primary, secondary and tertiary levels, better access to quality health services, the basic conditions for the suitability of premises and equipment will be ensured, which belongs to these services. The budget shall provide the required requirements for action in the event of natural disasters. Social health care is provided by the State through public funds for public health activities carried out by institutions and institutes. With both draft budgets of the Republic of Slovenia, the government has indicated strategically planned moves towards a way out of the crisis, which are most reflected in the cuts in social and pension resources, science and education. Education needs to be emphasized here, which is not a good indicator. Miracles and faster improvement after years of wrong decisions in this unstable international environment cannot be realistically expected. Therefore, the provision of solidarity, social security and compassionate care for the fellow human being and equal opportunities should be a priority for the country rightly expected by the citizens. Members and Members of the Group of the Democratic Party of Pensioners of Slovenia are aware of the importance of the early adoption of the two supplementary draft budgets of the Republic of Slovenia. In the Parliamentary Group, we know that if the adoption of both budget acts in the Republic of Slovenia were refused, there would be a situation that would prevent normal working conditions for the public sector, without wages, pensions, social transfers. In a word, the state would stop. In the Parliamentary Group of the Democratic Party of Pensioners of Slovenia, we will, aware of the severe consequences of the possible decline in budgetary acts, support the two complementary draft budgets of the Republic of Slovenia, in the hope that these are the last so-called crisis budgets and that we will discuss more positive guidelines at their rebalancing. Thank you.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-12-05-SDZ6-Izredna-23.ana.u7	On behalf of the New Slovenia Group, Mr Jožef Horvat.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-12-05-SDZ6-Izredna-23.ana.u8	Thank you very much, Mr. President. Dear fellow Members and fellow Members, Madam Minister, Madam Ministers, on behalf of the New Slovenia Group of Ambassadors, I welcome you very much! In the introduction, several basic dimensions of the two completed draft budgets of the Republic of Slovenia for 2013 and 2014. With the updated draft budget of the Republic of Slovenia for 2013, the revenues amount to €8,611 billion and €9,620 billion. The deficit will therefore be EUR 1,009 billion. In the 2014 updated draft budget of the Republic of Slovenia, revenue is projected at EUR 8,409 billion and expenditure at EUR 9,317 billion. The deficit will still be close to €0.908 billion. The budget of the Republic of Slovenia for 2013 and 2014 and accompanying documents shall be adopted by the National Assembly in circumstances which are extremely difficult, more than ever after independence. Slovenia has become a country with the highest trend of public debt growth in Europe from a stable and growing country in recent crisis years, and the gains of fiscal expansion in the period 2009-2011 reflect the high price of missed opportunities. The real volume of gross domestic product in the country is now below the level from 5 years ago. After the gross domestic product per capita, expressed in purchasing power, we have lost almost a decade now at the level of 2003. The actual volume of investments fell even to a level from 13 years ago, to 1999. Employment in the country is at the level of 2005, which means we went back seven years. On the other hand, employment in the public sector with 160,000 employees is far the highest in our history. The deficit in the state sector remained high after a strong increase in the first crisis year 2009, as well as in the next two years, 2010 and 2011 and reached the highest level since 1995 last year. This is just a few facts that have put strong pressure on our public financial position to place more than 6% of the budget deficit in 2009, 2010 and 2011 in line with the increase in public debt in the amount of 21.9% of gross domestic product at the end of 2008 over the 50 % limit. In 2009 to 2011, we have doubled our public debt. More than €8 billion in 2009-2011 debt was ultimately reflected in one billion gross domestic product and close to 50,000 additional unemployed instead of higher economic growth, investment and development. Today it is not intended to answer the question where all this money went, but there will be plenty of opportunities for that. Slovenia has faced a crisis with some structural problems, as well as the highest level of economic development in its history and as the second least indebted member of the euro area. The gross domestic product per capita, expressed in purchasing power, reached 91 % of the European Union average in 2008. With the exception of Luxembourg, we were the only member of the European Monetary Union with a public debt of below 22% of gross domestic product. We also had the lowest number of unemployed in our history. We had a practically balanced state budget. However, Slovenia has also experienced a number of structural weaknesses. The extremely rigid regulation of the labour market, the high taxation of labour, the low flow of foreign direct investment, the closed economy, the low efficiency of tertiary education and investment in applied research and development, the inefficient management of state assets and some other problems arising from pre-crisis times have prompted the slow adjustment of our economy to completely new and difficult circumstances. Due to the unpreparedness of the Slovenian economy and the poor business environment, the Slovenian economy fell to the dramatic 20 cities on the competitiveness scale in 2010 and Slovenia won an infamous record among the countries observed this year. At this turbulent time, when we have to listen to the talk of at least a dignified part of the street, which is aimed at all three branches of state authority, including local authorities, I am disappointed to note that we are not able to come together for the interests of the state. The light exception was unanimously adopted yesterday as a pension reform. This raises the question of what is actually happening to us in Slovenia. It is true that in 21 years of our independence we have achieved something. It is true that Slovenia was very important at some point, a new star in the European sky, but we probably never answered the questions after 25 June 1991 where we came from, where we are and where we are going. I agree with the connoisseurs that in euphoria, we have simply painted our own independent state's facade, replaced the flowers on the window shelves, but we have not ventilated the house and thoroughly rearranged its interior. That's why it's not functional today, it's outdated and it's stale. Back to the budget. We are considering the first budgets of the Republic of Slovenia prepared by this government, which the National Assembly gave it in February this year, and it is also a government whose organisational scheme is the smallest since we have our own independent country. We have the smallest government because of greater efficiency, but also because of lower costs. Reducing public spending and in this context all public expenditure is one of the key priorities of the government and the coalition. Thus, the Government took the first step in reducing the costs of the state immediately, at the very beginning of its mandate, by reducing the number of ministries. Only savings on the salaries of ministers and secretaries at the annual level amount to almost half a million euros. I provide some data showing savings in the functioning of the State Administration. According to data from the ministries, the wage for employees in ministries paid in July 2011 is lower by €3.85 million, a savings of €46.2 million at the annual level. The number of copyright and business contracts has also decreased. If there was a total of 1161 last July and the total monthly cost on the basis of these contracts was EUR 368,000, it was EUR 783 in July of this year, and the monthly payment was EUR 218 000. Compared to July last year and July this year, the reduction in the cost of copyright and business contracts at the monthly level amounted to around 150 000 euros, an estimated savings of 1.8 million euros at the annual level. The comparison of the cost data for official travel in government institutions in the first half of this year compared to the same period last year shows significant savings since it was used for 2.7 million euros less than last year. The 2013 and 2014 draft supplementary budgets of the Republic of Slovenia also follow the objectives of the Treaty for Slovenia 2012-2015 and the 2015 objective of the balanced budget of the Republic of Slovenia. Whether the time interval at which we want to balance the state budget is too short is a question. I'm sure we have different concepts. These concepts were also addressed by the outgoing President of the Republic and, if I understand it correctly, he proposed a balance of public finances, a reduction in budgetary expenditure at a longer time interval. At first glance, a nice solution, which, unfortunately for our understanding, also means extending the waiting time to improve credit ratings. This means that we should extend the waiting time for more favourable loans, extend the waiting time for investments and eventually extend the waiting time for new jobs to solve the acute existential problems of our citizens. This is not what we agree to in New Slovenia. I would also like to point out here that the Board of Directors of the IMF, the International Monetary Fund, assessed the coalition strategy in Slovenia as appropriate, particularly its awareness of the urgency of rapid action. In his report on Slovenia, the IMF called for a rapid implementation of the reform plan, including a balance of public finances. This call for action seems to me to be very important. The IMF accompanied the consideration of the report on Slovenia by a statement supporting the Slovenian Government's efforts to balance public finances and implement structural reforms. The IMF noted that the Slovenian economy is facing so-called negative recessional backlogs when problems in certain areas are exacerbating problems in other areas. Such negative effects work during the banking deleveraging process and with the financial difficulties of over-indebted companies, and are further encouraged by the structural weaknesses of the Slovenian economy, the fragility of the Slovenian financial system, weak domestic consumption and declining export demand. All of this, according to the IMF, is still calling for decisive and immediate action to address these problems. The IMF has assessed the strategy of the current ruling coalition, which focuses on measures to balance public finances, as appropriate, while welcoming plans for comprehensive and ambitious structural reforms. In the IMF, measures to remedy public finances have been supported so far, mainly based on a reduction in public spending. They are convinced that this was necessary to strengthen the confidence of financial markets. When we talk about reducing public spending, we, at least the government and the coalition MPs, often testify to the likelihood that we are overthrowing the social state, that we have stolen the social state, that we have brought the people into misery and could list them. The set of similar demagogic and yawning expressions is very broad. I would like to explain further and demonstrate by the figures that budgetary resources to ensure an adequate level of social state have increased over the past decade in the Republic of Slovenia. One of the key priorities of this government is to ensure an adequate level of social state, which is also based on the 2013 and 2014 draft budget. To compare, I will provide data on specific social items from 2002 and from the 2013 draft budget that we are adopting today. The figures show that, at the time of the 46% increase in tax revenues in 2002, the level of social items in the budget has increased above-proportionally. As a result, unemployment contributions to poor tenths of the 2013 unemployment rate will be 2.5 times higher, or 143 percent higher, than they were in 2002. Social security funds have doubled over the past decade to nearly €300 million. However, 55% will also be higher funding for the implementation of family policy. Let's see the numbers. Social security: implementation 2002, 158 million, draft budget 2013, 296 million – 87% increase. Family policy: implementation 2002, 393 million, draft budget of the Republic of Slovenia for 2013, 610.5 million – increase by 55%. Unemployment transfers: implementation 2002, 106 million, budget 2013, 258 million – increase by 143 %. Active employment policy measures: implementation of the 2002 budget, poor 60 million, draft budget for 2013, poor 97 million – increase by 64%. It follows from the above that the Government of the Republic of Slovenia continues to pay particular attention to the issue of the social state with its proposed budget 2013. In order to be able to pursue this objective in Slovenia in the future, urgent reforms need to be adopted. The reform package, in addition to pension reform and labour market reform, also covers the Slovenian State Holding Act, the Banking Stabilisation Act and individual packages to promote a more favourable business environment with less administrative burdens. Only with the timely implementation of all the measures listed above will economic development and the consequent increase in employment, a halt to the downward trend in pensions and young people be able to create new and safer jobs. As a result of the crisis and the approach to resolving the crisis, investments or funds for them have decreased in recent years. We all know that investments are essential for economic recovery and that is why we are pleased in New Slovenia that in the budgets for the next two years we are significantly increasing the investment index relative to wages and material costs. At a time when these investments were urgently needed, Slovenia had poorly invested the available European funds from the existing financial perspective. Many of these funds are still available and this is the main source of investment. In addition to the adoption of the budget of the Republic of Slovenia for the next two years, we need to realize something else in Slovenia, given the insufferable ease of referendum blockades, much more difficult tasks. Without the rehabilitation of the Slovenian banking system, it is not possible to count on positive economic growth, even if we do everything else. It is not even possible to predict what will happen to companies that today are in difficulty due to loans that cannot be repaid in time, otherwise they have good programmes, new products and new markets. The current model of crisis resolution in the banking sector has proved to be ineffective. It's a recapitalisation model. During the last three years, Slovenian taxpayers, through a decision of the previous government, which misjudged the situation in Slovenian state banks, directed new and new hundreds of millions to state banks. Banks are still producing losses and at the same time we have banks in Slovenia that are not state-owned, which have operated under the same conditions of tight economic conditions and which create profits, and we have not recapitalised them with state money. The model of recapitalisation of banks proved to be ineffective and expensive for Slovenian taxpayers. For the second time, according to the Council of State, the National Assembly has approved the Act on measures taken by the Republic of Slovenia to strengthen the stability of banks. Unfortunately, there is a referendum fate hanging over him. The resolution of the banking system will not automatically ensure the release of the credit crunch. We agree with this in New Slovenia. Many Slovenian companies are over-indebted and without an adequate share of property to allow additional credit for development. The rehabilitation of the banking system is necessary, but it is not sufficient. The country's capital investments should also be an important budgetary resource. Their management is dispersed between 5 different institutions. This management is ineffective, expensive and opaque. It happens that different managers of the same state investment decide in the assemblies quite the opposite, even in conflict and irrational ways. At a time when some live on the social floor, we are witnessing high awards, long-term contracts are signed, jobs are threatened and no one is responsible. The interconnection of private interests between members of the administration and the supervisory boards, which is a shift from good corporate governance practices, is also a major problem. All of this we wanted to end and create order with the solutions that we have written in the Slovenian State Holding Act, since the current situation systematically reduces the value of state assets and discourages the interest of foreign capital after investing in Slovenian companies. Unfortunately, there is also a referendum fate over this law. Members and Members of Parliament know exactly what measures are crucial for Slovenia at this time. This is not only the position of the coalition, it is also the official position of the European Commission, the European Central Bank, the International Monetary Fund and the OECD, the four non-foreign institutions are ours because Slovenia is a part of each of them. Their opinion is very important and, among other things, has an impact on the country's credit ratings and on the value of our state assets or how much Slovenia is still a relevant and valued political and economic partner in the world. Dear Members and Members of New Slovenia will be supported by the 2013 and 2014 draft budget of the Republic of Slovenia. We could be overplaying different draft budgets and allowing for even greater budget deficits, but we are aware that if we want to maintain sovereignty, we need to manage our indebtedness. ParlaMint-SI_2012-12-05-SDZ6-Izredna-23.ana.u16	Thank you for your word and a nice greeting on behalf of the Group of Social Democrats. The Parliamentary Group notes that the 2013 and 2014 budgets should provide an answer to address the situation in Slovenia. Messages at peaceful meetings are clear. We need a stable economy, jobs, public education, health care. This is not sufficiently taken into account in the draft budgets. Fast saving is not the answer, the sale of state property in the best companies is not, either, the privatisation of health care, and in particular not the destruction of public education for the promotion of private education. The fact is, gross domestic product is falling. According to data from the Statistical Office in the last quarter by 3.3%. For the first time, exports are falling by 0.7%. These are the data to which the Government must react, as they show that Slovenia is again in recession. The government and the coalition must begin to listen to the hardships of the people. Obviously, neither the government nor the coalition are yet able to do this, because in the messages we are listening to, the President of the Government wants to change the political system, submit to the judiciary, parliament, resolve mayors, Members of Parliament, and invite us to compete on who will make more proposals. If the situation in the judicial branch of the government, the legislative branch of the government and local self-government are dealt with in this way, legal certainty in Slovenia will certainly be reduced. All of this can be done without hindrance and in a thoughtful way that in the future we will not cause a competition in how many judges will be replaced if the policy is not satisfied with the judgment delivered. The fact is that the social situation of people is getting worse. Therefore, the Social Democrats once again warn the Government that it will cease its predatory interests in the sale of 12 billion state assets and will abandon the most expensive form of bank rehabilitation of EUR 5 billion and will begin to deal with job conservation and job creation measures. The fact is that at the very moment that we are discussing budgets for 2013 and 2014, the Government is facing a collapse of the Capital Investment Management Agency, which is responsible for managing 9 billion state assets. The President or the Acting President of the Agency's Board of Directors resigned. We hear that a statement of resignation yesterday was also made by members of the agency’s board or board of agencies. This is a complete government failure in the area of state property management. How can you expect the income situation to improve in the budget? How can you expect these companies to operate better, to keep jobs and to be a gross domestic product growing? The Chamber of Commerce of Slovenia predicts the possibility of maintaining 10,000 jobs, opening up 30 thousand new jobs. They predict an increase in exports of €1.5 billion. We ask the government how it will act and respond to the economic hand offered. Or by changing the political system or by following the economic deadlines offered? Social Democrats warn that tightening the belt in every way is not the answer. There is no international confidence that the Government will reduce the deficit below 3% of gross domestic product in 2013. These are assessments by the OECD, the European Commission and the International Monetary Fund. Obviously, the government's thinking needs to be changed. For example, in the European Commission, Spain negotiated a reduction in the deficit below 3% of gross domestic product from 2013 to 2014. Why does Slovenia strongly insist on a policy of acute saving, reducing social security for people and declining economic growth? These are questions that these two budgets do not answer. When we look at the fiscal policy of the government, we find that the government has decided to make the biggest fiscal effort in 2012. Given the situation in the economy, this is the worst solution at the worst possible time. This has caused the government itself to decline economic growth to the greatest extent. As a result, we warned and referred to the analysis of the International Monetary Fund on rapid savings, which poses a major threat to economic recovery. In 2012, the International Monetary Fund acknowledged that it underestimated the impact of fiscal consolidation on economic growth. Therefore, the value of the fiscal multiplier is between 0,9 and 1,7 which means that the reduction of the structural deficit by 1,9 % of the gross domestic product in 2012 results in a fall of the gross domestic product from 1,71 % to a maximum of 3,2 % of the gross domestic product. The Minister of Finance also explained on the Committee that the structural deficit in 2013 was expected to decrease by 1.5% of gross domestic product, which means an additional decline or economic downturn in 2013, even to a lower level than it was in 2009. The impact of the whole government policy on total consumption, both in public and in private, needs to be looked at. The government has reduced the real disposable income of public servants, recipients of social transfers and pensioners through measures taken by the Public Finance Balance Act. It also increased the number of unemployed in 2012. The President of the Government, in his statements on the Greek scenario, created a hysteria of threats and intimidation, further deepening the crisis by reducing Slovenia's credit ratings and, consequently, encouraging the hiring of new loans or the deterioration of the conditions for reprogramming existing loans. In the history of Slovenia, consumption falls for the first time in 2012. This is a fact that is statistically justified. Public and private consumption is falling in 2012. According to the autumn 2012 European Commission's assessment, the decline in private and public consumption will result in a 2.4% reduction in economic growth in 2012 and 1.6% in 2013. If the government did not implement the policy of intimidation, the economic policy of rapid savings, economic growth could even be positive in 2012 and 2013. We would also maintain a higher level of employment, which is what people in Parliament expect us to do. Slovenia has the third largest drop in gross domestic product in 2012 in the euro area, with the third largest drop in gross domestic product in the euro area in 2013. The cause of the fall of gross domestic product is no longer the external crisis, the global crisis, but we can look for many reasons within Slovenia. The consequences of saving the country and its impact on the income of the population through the Public Finance Balance Act are increasingly clear. The decline in domestic demand in 2012 and 2013 is among the highest in the euro area. This is reflected in expenditure data for both private consumption and country spending data. In 2012, the activity of the export-oriented economy, which is expected to grow by 0.1% in 2012, and 1.9% in 2013, is also worrying. Let me remind you that exports increased by 7% in 2011! The decline in exports by 0,3 % in the second quarter of 2012 and in the third quarter by 0,7 % of gross domestic product shows the seriousness of the economic situation. Measures will need to be taken to accelerate exports and the government in the budgets ignores this problem. We ask inside the Social Democrats whether that is why early elections were needed. In the Group of Social Democrats, we regret that the Government did not choose for 2012 a prudent and gradual consolidation of public finances that would not have acted against economic growth. The reduction of the deficit only at the expense of public spending in 2012 stifled the economy. We therefore stress that if the laws adopted in the previous term, which fell at the referendums, were to be adopted today, there would be no need for such a drastic reduction in public spending, only to extend the intervention law until the end of 2012 and to tax the wealthiest revenues more and things would be completely manageable. As we look at the revenue policy of the current government, we estimate that the continuation of the deficit reduction should be based on an increase in the revenue of the state budget. In order to achieve this, we have shown in the previous mandate when we wanted to reduce the grey economy, but then the policy encouraged the referendums, including the law on the prevention of black work, which would bring undeclared employment for workers. A little work would also reduce the grey economy. The fall of pension reform has increased the number of pensioners to record levels. As a result, the hole in 2012 is 300 million. As a result, there is a larger hole in the health treasury. When we look at the effects of the new tax proposals since 2013, we can say that the Bank's Financial Services Tax Act does not introduce taxes to collect more money from banks, but the banks will transfer this tax to positions, as all costs are passed on to end-users. Given the fact that the government is imposing this law on the bills, it will tax the weakest. It will also tax, to the maximum extent, pensioners who do not use electronic banking services and pay post office and bank boxes where they charge the highest fees for bills. This proves that the burden of the crisis will again be on the weakest. With amendments to the Tax Law on Balanced Banks, the government additionally taxes banks and further deepens credit crunches – quite the opposite of what the economy needs. The amendments to the Income Act have resulted in the adoption of a vigilary taxation of rental income. Instead of progressive taxation, Cedular Income Taxation has been adopted and thus the Government is burdening the richest tenants who give out the most housing, and is burdening those who give out fewer apartments and are able to resolve their social situation. We welcome the fact that we have helped to put a stop to the senseless proposals of the Minister of Finance regarding amendments to the Value Added Tax Act, but at the same time we believe that we must be extremely careful in the next steps as to how this resource will be replaced. The proposal for a value added tax law provided for 70 million more tax revenues. Instead, the government is supposed to raise excise duties on fuels, excise duties on alcohol and cigarettes, double the excise duty on electricity. They're also supposed to impose a tax on sweet drinks. We think that this way of taxation can be problematic, especially the doubling of excise duties on electricity. While the minister says that there will be only one euro in the positions, this increase will affect the most socially sensitive. It can also be problematic to raise excise duties on electricity for companies by the Government. This increase will threaten the labour intensive sectors, which are then very quickly threatened with job loss. Therefore, the Social Democrats propose that the Government cancel the reduction of the corporate income tax in 2013 by €190 million rather than raising electricity costs. With regard to the tax on sweet drinks, we need to be careful because this encourages drinking with artificial sweeteners that are even more harmful to health. All these measures are also opposed by the Chamber of Commerce of Slovenia, which strongly rejects the proposal to introduce higher excise duties in energy consumers who, in their view, are already too high and could further jeopardise the competitiveness of the Slovenian industry. They also oppose the introduction of a sweet drink tax because customers will thus buy cheaper drinks with sweeteners and dyes. The recession in which the Slovenian industry is located does not allow the increase of excise duties on energy products. For some companies this may even be dangerous, as the increases in already high and therefore non-competitive excise duties on energy products may no longer be tolerated, they point out at the Chamber of Commerce of Slovenia. In their view, the measure is selective and may have similar negative effects in particular sectors, such as a selective increase in value added tax. They therefore propose that the Ministry of Finance immediately withdraw the proposed increase in excise duties on energy products and the introduction of a tax on sweet drinks. The minister is expected to move excise duties on energy products down to the level of the average of the European Union, not up. They also expect the proposed introduction of sweet drinks tax to be withdrawn because buyers will buy cheaper substitutes, and the purchase to the Treasury will be substantially less modest and not properly calculated, because they believe that the calculation was made at a time when economic growth and consumption were significantly higher than they are today. The revenue from excise duty on energy consumers in the share of gross domestic product is already the highest in comparison with the countries within the European Union. The Chamber of Commerce points out that higher excise duties will significantly reduce the competitiveness of the entire Slovenian industry and, consequently, we can expect lower exports again, we can expect a fall in gross domestic product and a re-loss of jobs. It is also important to stress within price policy that it is not only a problem of increasing excise duties on energy products but also a problem of easing prices for municipal services in municipalities. The fact is that prices of utilities are forecast to rise to up to 300%. Some even speak of a 400% increase in the prices of municipal services. Such a price policy is certainly not conducive to economic growth. This cost will impose a heavy burden on our industry, so the reduction in corporate tax rates, including an increase in tax reliefs, will reduce the tax liability for 2013 by €190 million, is certainly one of the solutions proposed by the Social Democrats. In 2014 and 2015, when the tax rate is reduced by one percentage point each year under the proposal, this is estimated to mean an additional EUR 32 million less tax each year, or a total of EUR 266 million less government revenue. The saddest part is that if the government maintains this tax at such a low rate, it will spend these EUR 266 million on direct consumption, not on raising economic growth and increasing employment. Companies will not invest further, despite being reduced by their tax liability or increasing tax reliefs within the corporate income tax. In a situation where orders are decreasing, companies are reducing production and reducing activity. This is why they do not decide on new investments. The analysis by the Ministry of Finance on the effect of reducing the tax rate on corporate income by means of a general equilibrium model shows minimal effects on all elements of gross domestic product. In these uncertain circumstances, it would be better for the State to directly encourage the economy through subsidies for investment in technological equipment and calls for company development projects. In view of the content of the 2013 budget, we note that the Government continues to cut development funding. For this reason, we expect Slovenia to increase the development backlog compared to the European Union. We believe that the Government should maintain or even increase subsidies to encourage businesses. Over-indebted companies without State aid will not be able to help and stay. The data from Banka Slovenije show that only one third of companies today are able to repay loans. The recovery of the economy in the 2009-2011 mandate could be faster, but economic growth has further fallen due to the consequences of the previous wrong economic policy that overheated the economy. Since the beginning of 2005, economic growth has been based on increased indebtedness. The most pronounced increase in gross external debt in 2007 was a good EUR 10 billion, which means that 6.8% of economic growth was based on very unhealthy foundations. The total external gross debt increased from € 15.3 billion to €39.2 billion in the 2004-2008 mandate. This amount is the same as the amount owed by Slovenia from independence until the end of 2004. Thus, Slovenia became a net debtor instead of a net creditor. In 2004, we still had €2 billion left, and in 2008 we were already in debt of €13 billion. With the return of this government, the rapid consolidation of public finances in 2012 has resulted in a permanent loss of economic growth potential. We estimate that it made the same mistakes made by the Japanese government in 1997 and 2001, when it was saving to a greater extent, without taking account of the situation in the economy. We can talk about losing a whole decade, not a few years of successful economic development. What is the direction in the expenditure of the state budget when we look at the comparison between the previous and the current government? The Statistical Office of the Republic of Slovenia published the first provisional data on the development and research activities of Slovenia in 2011. According to this data, our national economy invested €894 million or 2.47% of gross domestic product in research and development in 2011. This is a very important indicator of the development intensity of the national economy and the importance of knowledge in its competitiveness on the world market and its acquired comparative advantages. Knowledge has a bearing on the employment, well-being and prospects of the entire national economy. Slovenia, with a 2.5% share of gross domestic product in R & D, is immediately classified as developing intensive countries, which target around 3% of gross domestic product or even more for these investments. The Scandinavian countries, with the exception of Norway, Japan and South Korea, have an investment share in research and development of up to 3% of their gross domestic product and the United States of America, Germany and Austria have around 3% of their gross domestic product. In 2011, Slovenia made a significant leap ahead of the average of the European Union — France, the Netherlands, the United Kingdom — not to mention other countries. It is therefore noted that in 2011 in Slovenia companies contributed a large part of funding for R & D investments. At the same time, the majority of these investments were made in the business sector, up to 74%. These percentages are not significantly different from other economically developed countries. The increased development intensity of Slovenia in 2011 did not come on its own. It has been enabled and encouraged by a number of measures of our economic policy, ranging from tax reliefs for R & D investments, development credits from the Slovenian Export Company and the European Investment Bank, to state grants mainly linked to the absorption of various European Union funds. Between 2008 and 2011, investment in research and development in Slovenia increased by €277 million and 45%. In 2011 business sector investments were 37% and the country's investments were 108% higher than, for example, in 2008. As we look at the situation in today's government, one year after the early elections, we note that the 2013 and 2014 draft budgets continue to be a policy of decommissioning science and information society, which started with the 2012 rebalancing. The reduction in resources for science and the information society shows the government's priorities compared to the previous government. The former government has realised resources for science and the information society amounting to €293 million; the 2012 budget balance has reduced to around €209 million. The 2013 budget proposal reduced them to EUR 211 million and the 2014 budget to EUR 177 million. How will we follow the proposals for investment in R & D of 4% of gross domestic product, which is also suggested by our most prominent economic expert, Dr. Peter Kraljič, if we want to reduce Slovenia's development backlog? With such a drastic reduction in R & D resources as are recorded in these two budgets, it certainly does not. Slovenia ranked 6th in 2010 after investing in R & D, at 2% of gross domestic product and Finland at first, with almost 4% of gross domestic product, are comparable after the crisis to the country with the largest drop in gross domestic product in 2009 and with the export attitude of the economy. If we look today at where Finland is, where Slovenia is, we can seriously ask ourselves what we are doing or what the ruling policy is doing right now. Reducing research and development resources will certainly result in the loss of the most educated people. These will go abroad and I think we are already dealing with this fact. The funds for young researchers were almost €43 million in 2011. The 2012 balance reduces to €31 million and in 2013 to €25 million. Young researchers flee abroad for this reason. The consequences of this government's actions — I hope we are aware — will therefore be permanent — permanently negative. Research projects are being phased out and scientific research activities are dying out. From 210 million in 2011, we come to 153 million in 2012 and to 143 million euros in 2013. The reduction in budgetary resources and other measures envisaged by the Government in the field of education, science and research will, in the long term, reject the intellectual potential of this country and put the institutions implementing the programmes at the brink of survival. Therefore, it is not random to warn experts that compare current developments with events in systems that are supposed to exceed them civilisationally and, in themselves, offer a thesis on the attack on the intellectual core of society. It is significant that new, political and economically semi-private higher education institutions are forecasting in the face of apparent public education poverty, with more or less clear that they rely primarily on public funds or the government. Increasing the number of children in classes, closing schools, reducing accessibility to quality public education and introducing schooling, impoverishment of public universities and other educational institutions, grading of teaching and research professions are only part of the envisaged measures that the Government takes into account all its documents it provides to us. It is clear from everything that universities and research institutes cannot bear such a drastic reduction in resources in 2013 and will therefore have a very serious situation. Failure to pay and leave or prepare for the departure of the best experts will stop the work process, illiquidity will result in the loss of services, all of which will divert attention and action away from teaching and research work. All direct and indirect consequences will be met mainly by young people who want to learn and want to shape their future with knowledge. If the Government would like to take care of the economy in order not to stifle it, it should maintain the level of R & D resources, maintain investment in education and the level of funding for active employment policy already in 2012. We are therefore proposing amendments to mitigate all these consequences and proposing an increase of at least €16 million in funding for the implementation of the first and second Bologna rates. We also propose to provide additional food resources of 25 million and to provide an additional EUR 10 million for the labour market. These are the amendments with which we want to mitigate at least a little bit all the consequences arising from the budgets adopted if they are adopted. If we link the 2013 and 2014 budgets with the law on the implementation of the budget, the policy or policy of this government is further exposed. The government wants more jurisdiction. The National Assembly is no longer supposed to decide on budget rebalancing. The purpose of the sale of assets is very clear from these budgets. In the law on the implementation of the budget, we can see that you want to sell 3.4 billion euro of the assets of the Republic of Slovenia that it has in the companies. You also want to transfer the responsibility in the law on the implementation of the budget to the directors of public institutions and even punish them if they are dealing with a loss. We therefore estimate that the Government, through the Law on the Implementation of the 2013 and 2014 budgets of the Republic of Slovenia, will sell out the last property of pensioners provided in Kad. The capital company will also become insolvent due to exhaustion, will be forced to sell its entire assets, almost 10% stake in Krk, 8.27% stake in Petrol, 5.6% stake in Telekom, 22.2% stake in Gorenje, 20.32% stake in Hit. All we can expect is that the Capital Society will be shut down as soon as it sells out. To a large extent, this will be known mainly in the financing of the Institute for Pension and Disability Insurance. The law on the implementation of the budget also gives a very strong mandate to allocate funds in the budgets by abolishing the Institute of Budget Rebalance and thus, with respect for colleagues and colleagues, the supervision of the National Assembly over government, the implementation of the budget. Public institutions also lose the part of their independence that they had under the applicable legislation, namely the preparation and adoption of a final account. Instead of increasing autonomy, the administration of the state in these public institutions is increasing. The Councils of the Institutions shall lose their importance in the work relating to financial operations. The required reduction in the level of wage funds, as foreseen by both budgets, will be achieved through various measures that will certainly affect the scope and quality of the public service in a given area. In view of the provisions of the Wages Act and the Public Finance Balance Act, it is expected that this can only be achieved in certain areas by amending the Staff Regulations or, if you wish, by forgiving. With regard to debt in the coming years and what we have heard, that this government is holding on to policies to reduce debt, it must be made clear that the debt over these two years is projected at EUR 9.1 billion, which is more than a billion over 2010 and 2011. How many eyewitnesses did we then hear that the former government is only in charge of Slovenia and that it is not thinking about how to solve problems? We note that such indebtedness raises doubts about the serious intention to reduce public debt. In this context, the appropriateness of the drafted text of the fiscal rule is again raised under questions, as debt would have increased significantly despite the reduction of the deficit. The fiscal rule, however, aims to reduce debt. So the government's action goes in a completely different direction than expected under the fiscal rule. The consolidation of public finances by 2015 and the abolition of the structural deficit for 2015 is wrong. Data from the European Commission and the International Monetary Fund show that economic growth and employment must be promoted by 2017. We do not see this in these budgets. With regard to the proposals for reforms and the resolution of the banking system, it is worth mentioning that the Social Democrats had already warned at the beginning of this government that it is necessary to approach the rehabilitation of banks – this shows the experience we have gained during the rule period – but not that fiscal consolidation is carried out as a first priority and the stability of banks as a last one. Nevertheless, we think that without lending there will be no growth and no exit from the crisis. We also agree with the assessment of external experts or experts in banking. For example, Mr Aljoša Tomaž, former director of the Bank, points out that the bad bank is significantly more expensive and less effective than the direct capital strengthening of banks. The country must maintain its stable financial system as it has shown in this crisis that foreign banks and branches of foreign banks are among the first to reduce lending. The financial crisis has shown how important banks are in financing economic development. The banks are the backbone of the economy and therefore there is a clear difference between the old, developed members of the European Union and the new members of the European Union. The old members of the European Union have maintained the majority of the banking system for financing the economic development of their country. The new members, with the exception of Slovenia, have left the banking market to foreigners and the financial crisis has revealed the weaknesses of foreign ownership. It is a question of the link between foreign ownership of banks and the financial stability of the country in which these banks operate. Through the privatisation and sale of the New Ljubljana Bank, foreigners will decide the fate of the property of the New Ljubljana Bank and also the property of all of us, because Nova Ljubljanska Banka is the second largest owner of the group Pivovarna Laško, the third largest owner of Petrol, the second largest owner of Istria Autoelectrica with 2 thousand 500 employees and the second most important owner of Istrabenz, Unior and the largest individual owner of the spa in Laškem. Also, Nova Ljubljanska Banka is the most important owner of a number of companies to be hit by the bankruptcy of church holding companies. The most important company among them is definitely the Helios Domžale Group with Belinka, which employs 2 000 536 people, or the Youth Book, which employs thousands of 500 people. Thus, with the announced sale of the country's share in the New Ljubljana Bank, the government will cause negative consequences and, in particular, the relocation of liquidity shocks from foreign countries to Slovenia, which can only be prevented by the majority ownership of the State, say, in the New Ljubljana Bank. Banka Slovenije will also lose some of its control over Nova Ljubljanska banka when it becomes, say, a branch or a subsidiary bank of another foreign bank. In doing so, the Bank of Slovenia will lose control of its financial stability and, respectable, we lose our sovereignty, which you mention so many times in your performances. In view of all the above, it is really important to ask why a bad bank is being set up. Why wouldn't it be better to recapitalise banks, say, from 800 million to 1.2 billion euros? Why don't we just solve the problems right now and not put this off for 2017, which can be fatal for us? We welcome the Government's action taken by Cimos and decided to recapitalise. We have also pointed out this in other companies, with the Government always voicing itself that Santa Claus will not help the economy. Despite the amendments proposed and hoped to be adopted by the Social Democrats, the overall budget composition for 2013 and 2014 is so problematic that we cannot support them. But we hope that our amendments will be adopted. Thank you.
